No, doc, no. There are no side effects in my routine. Everything is intentional. You don't just accidentally drop weight like you misplaced your car keys. It's a pursuit. It's... it's about maintaining absolute, unwavering control. \n\nI mean, if you want to understand exactly how deliberate this is, you just have to look at my mornings. Every single morning, 0430 hours, before the coffee, before the shower, before the uniform even comes out of the closet, I step on that scale. It's right there in the master bathroom, and I keep it on the exact same spot on the tile every single day so the calibration doesn't get thrown off by an uneven floor. You have to eliminate the variables, right? And I stand there, and I watch that digital display settle. \n\nAnd if that number is up... I mean, doc, if it's up even half a pound. Just a fraction of a pound! I get this literal, physical jolt. It’s like... well, it's like when you're sitting in the cruiser and the radio suddenly cracks open with an 'officer needs assistance' call. It's just this sudden, icy spike of panic right in my chest, this overwhelming feeling like I've completely lost control of the situation. Like the perimeter is breached. \n\nAnd I just can't have that. I absolutely cannot stand the idea of putting weight back on, of slipping backwards into that sloppy complacency I was telling you about earlier. It's entirely unacceptable to me. So if I see that half-pound increase, that's it. The whole day shifts into a different gear. I'll spend the rest of the shift cutting back even harder than my usual baseline, recalculating the intake, maybe skipping the authorized items I'd normally allow myself, just doing whatever it takes to regain that command of the situation. Because the thought of those numbers creeping back up, of losing my edge... it's a failure of discipline. So, no, it's not some passive side effect of how I eat. It's an active, daily, highly monitored operation to make sure I never put that weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any medical conditions or been on medications that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Medical conditions? No, absolutely not. It’s actually funny you ask that, because that is exactly where the department’s medical staff went right off the bat. When I had that screening I was telling you about, they didn't just stop with the nurse and the scale. They had the precinct doctor come in, and he spent... I don't know, it felt like an hour, just grilling me. He's asking, \"Are you on any new prescriptions? Any history of thyroid issues? Gastrointestinal problems?\" He drew a whole panel of blood—checked my thyroid levels, my blood sugar, all of it. \n\nAnd you know what they found? Absolutely nothing. Clean as a whistle. I am fifty-seven years old, doc, and I am not on a single daily medication. You look at the other guys my age on the force, the guys I came up with, and half their lockers look like a pharmacy. They’re popping statins for their cholesterol, blood pressure pills, diabetes medication, all because they spend their night shifts eating garbage in their cruisers and letting themselves go. But me? I don't even like taking an ibuprofen when my knees ache after standing on a perimeter for six hours in the cold. I just don't put that stuff in my body. \n\nSo, no, there is no underlying disease, no prescription side effect causing this. People always want to find a medical excuse, you know? They want to point to a chart and say, \"Oh, it's a glandular thing,\" or \"It's the medication,\" because they just cannot comprehend that a man can simply exercise total, absolute discipline over his own body without some biological interference. They want a pill or a sickness to blame it on so they don't have to look at their own lack of willpower. But there's no pill. There's no condition. It's just me, doing the work.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concerns about your weight or eating are related to how you see your body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Well, \"shape or size,\" I mean... yes, but it’s about what that shape represents. It’s about the standard, the command presence. You know, when I look in the mirror—and I have this full-length mirror on the back of the bedroom door, right where I get dressed for my shift every day—I don't see what everyone else says they see. \n\nThe nurse, the precinct doctor, the guys in the locker room, they look at me and they see whatever they want to see, someone who's \"dangerously low\" or whatever phrase they're using to justify their own panic. But I look in that glass and I don't see that at all. I'm looking for the discipline. I zero right in on my stomach and my waist. Because that midsection, doc, that is where a man shows his true colors. You look at these veteran cops, twenty, thirty years on the force, and their guts are literally spilling over their duty belts. It's a disgrace. It screams complacency, it screams that you've given up on the job and on yourself. \n\nSo I focus entirely on that area. I stand there in the morning, before the uniform goes on, and I scrutinize the waistline. And honestly? I judge my entire day—my whole outlook, my readiness for the shift, my entire mood—by whether I look lean enough in that mirror to actually respect myself. \n\nBecause if I don't look lean, if there's even a hint of softness or slack... how can I expect a suspect on the street or a citizen in crisis to respect my authority if I don't even have the self-respect to keep my own physical perimeter secure? It dictates everything. If I look sharp, if the waist is tight and the stomach is completely flat, then I'm squared away. The day is under control. I can put the badge on and do the work. But if I don't feel like I meet that standard, the whole day is compromised. It just eats at me. So, yes, it's absolutely about the shape, but it's because that shape is the only way I can measure if I'm still the man I demand myself to be.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining even the smallest amount of weight is driven by a concern about becoming fat, or is it more about what that weight gain represents to you personally?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's exactly like I was touching on a minute ago when I was telling you about my morning routine with the scale. It’s really both, because the two things are completely inseparable to me. Becoming fat *is* the failure. \n\nI mean, like I said before, if I step on that glass and the number is up even half a pound—just a fraction—I get this massive, physical jolt. It's this immediate, visceral panic like I've completely lost control of the wheel. And the reason I get that jolt isn't just because of the number itself, it's because of the trajectory. It's a slippery slope, doc. You let half a pound slide today, and you justify it. You say, \"Oh, it's just water weight,\" or \"I had a heavy meal.\" And then tomorrow it's a full pound. Next month it's five. Before you know it, you're one of those guys I was talking about, the ones who get winded just walking up a single flight of stairs to respond to a domestic disturbance call. \n\nI just absolutely cannot stand the idea of putting any of that weight back on. The very thought of it makes my skin crawl. So I react. I have to. If I see that spike, I will spend the entire rest of the day cutting back even harder than I already do. I'll tighten up the perimeter. I'll skip the black coffee, I'll ignore the hunger pangs during a twelve-hour shift, I'll recalculate every single thing I was planning to consume and I'll slash it. Because gaining weight, becoming fat... it represents a total collapse of personal discipline. It means you've surrendered to your own weakness. And in my line of work, and just in my life in general, surrender is not an option. So I clamp down. I clamp down harder until I know for an absolute fact that I am back in command.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel that your weight or body shape is distorted, or that you see yourself differently than how others describe you?\n\nPatient: Distorted? No, I wouldn't use that word. If anything, I think everybody else's vision is the one that's distorted. I mean, it goes right back to what I was just telling you about my routine with that mirror on my bedroom door. When I look in that glass, I absolutely do not see what everyone else claims they see. \n\nYou know, you get the guys in the locker room, or the department medical staff, and they’re pulling me aside, telling me my uniform is hanging off my shoulders, saying I look \"emaciated\" or whatever dramatic word they want to throw around to sound concerned. My sister called me just last week, practically in tears, telling me my face looks hollow. But I stand in front of that mirror, under the harsh light, and I just don't see it. Not even a little bit. \n\nI'm not looking at my face or my shoulders anyway. Like I said before, I focus right in on my stomach and my waist. That's the command center. And when I look there, I don't see someone who's wasting away—I see areas that still need tightening. I see the standard I have to maintain. I told you, I judge my whole day, my entire sense of self-worth, by whether that midsection looks lean enough for me to actually respect the man looking back at me. If there is any slack, if I can pinch anything at all, then I know I'm not there yet. \n\nIf people think my view is distorted, it's only because society has spent the last two decades surrounded by people who have completely let themselves go. They’ve normalized mediocrity and softness. So when they see someone who is actually disciplined, someone who refuses to carry a single ounce of unnecessary, sloppy baggage on his frame, it short-circuits their brains. They see a crisis because it makes them uncomfortable. I just see the reality of what it takes to keep myself sharp. So, no, I don't see myself differently because my mind is playing tricks on me. I see myself differently because I'm the only one actually looking at the truth."
  }
